Court and consequently, set aside the memos'

petjtioners. The State G f,vern ment,

)

4

5

6. Heard learned counsel for the petitioners, Mr. Harender

Goud, learned standing counsel for HMDA and Mr. K.R. Koteswara
Rao, learned standing counsel for Secunderabad Cantonment
Board.

6
7. Learned Speciai Government Pleader has taken preliminary
objection as to the maintainability of the writ petitions.
It is contended that the petitioners do nol have any locus standi to
institute these writ petitions. The claim of the petitioner!; that they
being residents of nearby area oF the subject land and that they
should be given preferential allotment of the subje(lt land is
misconceived and cannot form subject matter of adjudication under
Writ jurisdiction. The petitioners do not have any right much less
human right or l'undanrental right to claim allotment of particular
piece oI land, which suits tl.reir convenience. It is not si-ated as to
how the petitioners are having interes[ over the subject lan(!.
8. According tc the learned Special Governmenl- Pleader,
the petitioners are encroachers of the Gcverr'tment land in a colony
knourn as Sai Baba t-luts situated at Gurr Rock, .Ti[umalagiri,
Secunderabad. fhe plots in possession ol'tlre petitionei-s, for whrch
allegedly, D-Form pattas were issued, are not the sul)ject matter of
this /is. Thus, the petitioners do not have any locus standi to
challenge the impugr.red memos. It is further submitted that if the
petitloners approach the corrcerned authorities and if they are
eligible under any housing scheme of Governtrtent iil(e 2 BHK
scheme, their applications would be considered []enc€, the writ
petitions are misconceived and liable to be dismissed.
//i

1
9. The issue of locus standi came up for consideration before
the High Court of l(arnataka in WP.No.41717 of 2OtI and batch
dated 06.03.2013. The grievance of the writ petitioners -
M/s. Manyatha Residents Association, comprising of owners of
houses and sites in residential Iayout approved by the Bengaluru
Development Authority (BDA), was that areas earmarked for civic
amenities sites in the layout were illegally allotted to Gas Authority
of India Limited (GAIL), Syndicate Bank and others.
The learned Single Judge held that the writ petitioners had locus
standi lo challenge the allotment made in favour of the allottees
and quash the lease deeds executed in favour of the allottees ai
being in violation of Bengaluru Development Authority Act and the
Rules made thereunde r.
10. A Division Bench of the Karnataka High Court in WA.No.2872
of 2or3 and batch dated 26.O2.2021 while considering several
judgments of the Supreme Court on the point of locus standi made
the following observations:
43. In the context of frling a writ petition under Article 226
of the Constitution, it is well settled by several decisions of
the Hon'ble Suoreme court that only persons aggrieved can
l'ile writ petition in their personal / private capacity. In other
words, there must exist some legal or constitutional right
which has been violated or infringed and in such a case, a
person can ftle a writ Petltion.

8
44. The term "locus s'indt" ot "stdndtng to su.- denotell the
existence of a right of an individual or group of indrvidu€rls to
have a Court enter upon adjudication on an issue brought
beiore that Court by proceedings instituted by the individual
or the group of persons. The question of locus sltandi
assumes impcrtance when petitions are filed by incomP()tent
persons. Ordinarily, a lvrit petiticn can be filed by a p€rson
aggrieved and not by a st|anger except in public int(lrest
litigation and in the case of a wtii of quo-warranto.
45. Article 22€. of the Constitution does not lay down ils lo
who are tlre persons entitled to tnvoke the jurtsdiction of the
High Court under that Article. The question of locus standi
has been decided from ttme to time irr a large numbr:r of
cases. Generally, indrvidual pe|soi1 or a qroup of persons
agqrieved by any actron or inaction on the Part of the S;tatc
or its Authorities can institute a v',/rit petition under Ar-ticle
225 of tlre Constitution on the premise that, there ii an
infringement of their right or the)/ have been prejudi(:ially
affected by any order. Thus, the right which is the
foundation for exercising the jurisdiction under ,qrticle 2:16 of
the Constitution is tlle Personal or indivldual right of the
petitioner himselF, Lhough in the case of ra.rrits like, habeas
corpus c( quo-v,/arranto, this rttle stands modified
46. But, in the case of issuance of a writ o[ mandamus,
so as to compel the Authorities to do something, it mu:;t be
shown that tlte statute imposes a Iegal duty arrd the
aggrieved party has a legal right under the statut'l to
enl'orce its pertbrmance. But, where no right ot the
petitioner is aftected, such a person has no locus staniji to
file the writ petttlon. Thus, rlo person can ask for a
mandarrus without a legal right. There must be a judi(:ially
protected right before one suffcring a legal grievance can
ask for a martd.zrnus. A person c:tn be said to be aggrirlved
only,,vhen a person rs denied a iegal right by someone who

9
has a legal duty to do something or abstain from doing
something. Existence of the right is implicit for the exercise
of the extraordinary junsdiction by the Hrgh Court under
Article 226. For instance, a rival in a trade has no locus
standr to challenge the grant of licence to other trader on
the ground that the licence was granted illegally or suffers
from defect of jurisdiction, vide J.M.Desai vs. Roshan
Kumar, IAIR 7976 SC 578], (J.M.Desai); Nagpur Rice
and Flour Mills vs. Teekappa Gowda and Brothers,
IAIR 7977 SC 246] (Nagpur Rice and FIour Mills).
In ).M.Desai, provisions of Bombay Cinemas Registration
Act, 1953 and the Bombay Cinema Rules, 1954 came up for
consideratron and paragraphs 36 to 41 of the s;id judgment
read as u nder:
"36- It will be seen that in the context oF locus standi
to apply for a wnt of certiorari, an applicant may
ordinarily fall in any of these categories: (i) 'person
aggrieved'; (ii) 'stranger'; (iii) busybody oF
meddlesome interloper. Persons in the last category
are easily distinguishable From those coming under the
first two categories. Such persons interfere in things
which do not concern them. They masquerade as
crusaders fcr.;ustice. They pretend to act in the name
of Pro Bono Publico, though tlrey have no interest of
the public oT even of their own to protect. They
indulge in the past-trme of meddling with the judicial
process either by force of habit or from improper
motives. Often, they are actuated by a desire to win
notoriety or cheap popularity; y/hile the ulterior intent
of some applicants in this category may be no more
than spoking the wheels of administration. The High
Court should do well to reject the aoplicatrons of such
busy bodies at the threshold.

l0
37. The distinctron between the First and s(rcold
categories of applicant:i, though real, ls not alwa)/s
well-demarcated. The first category has, as it were,
two conccntr.ic zoncs; a solid central zone of certlinly,
and a grcy outer circle of lessening certainty in a
slidinq cent|ifugal scale, with an outermost nebulous
fringe of uncertainty. Applicants falling within tle
central zone are those whose legal rights have be,ln
infringed. Such applicants undoubtedly stand in tle
category of 'persons aggrieved'. In the grey 3ut€r
circle the bounds which separate the first category
from the second, intermix, interfuse and overl lp
increasingly in a centriftrgal direction. All persons in
tlrs outer-zc,ne may lrot be "persons aggrieved".
38. To drstrnguish sucl) aPplicants fron] 'strarrqerj',
among them, some boa:cj tests may be deduced from
the conspectus nlade above. These tests arc not
absolute and ultimate. Their etficacy varies accordilrg
to the circumstances of tlle case, including tl.le
statutory context in vrhich the matter falls to lle
consiclered. These are: Whether th3 applicant is a
person whose le-qal right fras been infringed? Has he

61. Day.io and day-out, the State, its instrumentalities aitd
agenctes would be engaged jn governance and in pass tg
variorrs orders. It is only when an action taken by the State, its instrumentalities or agencies or an order passed hy
them, which rvould have a direct efFect/irnpact on a persc,n
or a group of persons, then they could become aggriev€d
persons.rnd have the locus standi to move a writ petition
before ihe Hrgh Court under Artrcle 226 of the Constrtution.

62- The above is in contra -d istinction to a petition filed
purely in public interest. In the case of public interest
litigation, the petitioners would have no personal or
individual interest in the subject matter of the petitron. It is
filed purely in public interest so as to secuTe justice for
those who cannot approach the Court or in order to assail an
illegal action initiated by the State Government, its agencies
or instrumentalities. In such a case, doctrine of locus standi
is relaxed and the same is For the purpose of bringing to the
notrce ol'the Hrgh Court (or Supreme Court under Article 32
of the Constitution) for securing justrce, in tlre realm of
enforcement of fundamental rights, constitutional rights, or
other legal rights for the benefit of those persons who are
not in a position to approach the Constitutional Courts and
in the larger interest of the general public. In all other
cases, where a writ petition is [iled under Artrcle 226 of the
Constitution, for enforcement of a fundamental right or any
legal rrght, such a petitioner must have a Iocus standi to do
so by demonstrating that there is a legal wrong done to ltim
by violation of his right and therefore, being an aggrieved
person, has the right to file the writ petition and not
otherwise.
11, As evident irom the averments in the writ aFFidavits,
the grievance of the petitioners is that they had been waiting for
the past 50 years hoping that the Government would provide them
permanent shelter for their dwelling and instead of considering the
pliqht of the petitioners, the Government has chosen to allot
subject land to the respondents No.7 and B-polltical parties.
Though it is contended by the learned counsel for the petitioners
t.hat prime Government land is alienated l'or pittance and the same

is not in public interest, it needs to be notecJ that this rs ot a public
iriterest litiqation. petitioners conterrd that the lands at llowenpally
shoLrld be allotted to the petiiioners and that tlrey \vill have tirst
natural right to get dwellrng houses.
12. It is not disputed by the rearned counser for the petitioners
that the plots/lands in possession of the petitioners; are nct
proposed to be alienated by the Government under the impugned
memos. At the sante time, it is not the case of the petiti()ner.s that
atternpt is nrade by the resp.ndents to dispossess the oetitione.
from the land in their alleged occrtp;tiion. l-hus, the petitioners do
not have .rny foundational clarrn to instjtute these writ peUtions.
They cannot be said to be aggrieved bv the allotment under tne
impugned memos in any manner. A landless or homeles;s person
cannot claim that l-re shoukl be allott:ed land at a particu ar placc.
l-he allotmerrt of land under the impugnerj memos tn these wril
petitions ha9 no corrnection v,,hatsoever with the land/plots of the
oetitioners. l'hus, the petitioners do not have any locus .;tendi to
institut€ these writ petitions. Hence, the writ pctitions are
misconcerved, devoid of rnerits and Iiable to be dismissed.
